User: Is that guard on both merge paths?
Assistant: No — until 2026-08-07 it lived on EntityMerger only, which is the path that ships disabled. Creation-time resolution, the enabled one, had no guard. Both use blipshell/memory/entity_names.py now.

### Response

## 1. Primary Request and Intent
The user wants to **re-run the nightly entity merge** (which previously archived 7,491 entities) but is concerned about **version-numbered entities** (e.g., `projectecho_v1`, `projectecho_v2`) being incorrectly merged. They emphasize:
- **Preserving versioned entities** (e.g., `v1`, `v2` suffixes) to avoid unintended merges.
- **Strict adherence to the ARCHIVE-never-DELETE rule** (no deletions allowed).

- **Avoiding modifications to `cleanup_entities.py`** (as it performs hard deletes).
- **Ensuring proper cache invalidation** for entity names via `_invalidate_entity_name_cache` when writing entity rows outside the store’s methods.

---

## 2. Key Decisions Made
- **Re-run the merge** despite risks, with safeguards in place.
- **Disable deletion** (ARCHIVE-only, no DELETE operations).
- **Backup `data/blipshell.db`** before execution.
- **Use dry-run first**, then apply with `--loop` flag.
- **No changes to `cleanup_entities.py`** (hard-delete logic remains untouched).
- **Validate cache invalidation** for entity name writes.

---

- **Key files involved**:
  - `blipshell/memory/entity_names.py` (shared logic for both merge paths).
  - `merge_entities` (command to re-run; uses dry-run then `--loop`).
  - `cleanup_entities.py` (off-limits; contains hard-delete logic).
- **Critical guard logic**:
  - `entity_names.version_distinguished` (blocks merges where names differ by version/instance numbers).
  - **Missing guard in creation-time resolution path** (enabled path) until 2026-08-07 (previously only in disabled `EntityMerger` path).
- **Cache management**:
  - `_invalidate_entity_name_cache` must be called for external entity row writes.

## review-20260924T100941-0032 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-2

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

Why might an ESP32 with a MAX98357 I2S DAC produce noise or garbage audio? List the 3 most likely causes and how to debug each one.

### Response

1. **I2S Configuration Issues**
   - **Cause:** Incorrect bit depth, sample rate, or clock settings between ESP32 and MAX98357.
   - **Debug:**
     - Verify I2S settings match DAC specs (e.g., 16-bit, 44.1kHz, or 32-bit, 96kHz).
     - Check ESP32 I2S pins (BCK, WS, DOUT) and wiring (MAX98357 uses **WS=CLK, BCK=LRCLK, DOUT=DIN**).
     - Use an oscilloscope/logic analyzer to confirm signals.

2. **Power Supply Noise or Insufficient Voltage**
   - **Cause:** Unstable/low power (MAX98357 needs **3.3V ±5%**; ESP32 may need decoupling caps).
   - **Debug:**
     - Measure voltage at MAX98357’s **VDD** (should be clean, ~3.3V).
     - Add **10µF + 0.1µF** decoupling caps near DAC’s power pins.
     - Isolate power lines (avoid sharing with noisy sources).

3. **Ground Loops or Poor Grounding**
   - **Cause:** High-impedance or noisy ground paths (common in mixed analog/digital designs).
   - **Debug:**
     - Ensure **common ground** between ESP32 and MAX98357 (star grounding if possible).
     - Add **ferrite beads** or **LC filters** on audio lines if interference persists.
     - Test with a **direct digital source** (e.g., ESP32’s internal DAC) to isolate the issue.

## review-20260924T100941-0033 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-3

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

How do I connect an SSD1306 OLED to an ESP32?

### Response

Here’s the improved version:

---
To connect an **SSD1306 OLED** to an **ESP32**, follow these wiring steps:

1. **Power and Ground**:
   - Connect **VCC** to **3.3V** (ESP32’s logic level).
   - Connect **GND** to **GND**.

2. **I²C Pins** (most common configuration):
   - **SDA** → **GPIO 21** (default I²C SDA on ESP32).
   - **SCL** → **GPIO 22** (default I²C SCL on ESP32).
   - Ensure no other I²C devices conflict with these pins.

3. **Library Setup**:
   - Install the **Adafruit SSD1306** and **Adafruit GFX** libraries via Arduino Library Manager.
   - Alternatively, use **U8g2** for broader compatibility.

4. **Initialization Code**:
   ```cpp
   #include <Wire.h>
   #include <Adafruit_GFX.h>
   #include <Adafruit_SSD1306.h>

   #define SCREEN_WIDTH 128
   #define SCREEN_HEIGHT 64

   Adafruit_SSD1306 display(SCREEN_WIDTH, SCREEN_HEIGHT, &Wire, -1);

   void setup() {
     Wire.begin(21, 22); // SDA=21, SCL=22 (ESP32 default)
     if (!display.begin(SSD1306_SWITCHCAPVCC, 0x3C)) { // Common I²C address: 0x3C or 0x3D
       Serial.println(F("SSD1306 allocation failed"));
       while (1);
     }
     display.clearDisplay();
     display.display();
   }
   ```

**Notes**:
- Verify the OLED’s I²C address (often **0x3C** or **0x3D**; check datasheet).
- For SPI mode (faster but requires more pins), use **D0=GPIO14, D1=GPIO13, CS=GPIO15, DC=GPIO4, RST=GPIO16**.
- Ensure proper pull-up resistors (~4.7kΩ) on SDA/SCL if stability issues arise.

## review-20260924T100941-0034 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-4

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

For a single-user local assistant with 30k memories, compare a dual-store setup (SQLite + a separate vector DB) vs a single store (sqlite-vec inside the SQLite file). What's the main risk of the dual-store approach and when is it nonetheless justified?

### Response

### **Comparison: Dual-Store (SQLite + Vector DB) vs. Single-Store (sqlite-vec)**

#### **Dual-Store (SQLite + Vector DB)**
- **Pros:**
  - **Optimized for each use case**: SQLite handles relational data efficiently, while a dedicated vector DB (e.g., Qdrant, Milvus, or Weaviate) excels at vector similarity search.
  - **Scalability**: Vector DBs are often designed for high-dimensional data and can scale better for large embeddings or future growth.
  - **Flexibility**: Easier to swap out the vector DB if requirements change (e.g., switching from ANN to exact search).
  - **Concurrency**: Vector DBs often handle concurrent writes better than SQLite for embeddings.

- **Cons:**
  - **Consistency risk**: **Main risk**—data must be kept in sync between SQLite (metadata) and the vector DB (embeddings). If they drift (e.g., due to crashes, partial updates, or schema changes), the assistant may return stale or mismatched results.
    - Example: A memory is deleted in SQLite but not in the vector DB (or vice versa), leading to incorrect retrievals.
  - **Complexity**: Requires additional logic to manage sync (e.g., transactions, idempotent updates, or eventual consistency patterns).
  - **Overhead**: Extra network calls (if vector DB is separate) or process management (if embedded).

- **When justified?**
  - **High embedding volume**: If embeddings are large (e.g., >100k vectors) or require advanced indexing (e.g., HNSW, IVF), a dedicated vector DB may outperform sqlite-vec.
  - **Mixed workloads**: If the system will later need to support multi-user or distributed setups, a dual-store approach is more adaptable.
  - **Specialized vector ops**: Need for advanced features like dynamic filtering, hybrid search, or GPU acceleration for vectors.

---

#### **Single-Store (sqlite-vec)**
- **Pros:**
  - **Simplicity**: No sync overhead; atomic transactions ensure consistency.
  - **Performance**: Lower latency (no inter-process communication) and better for small-to-medium datasets (<100k embeddings).
  - **Portability**: Single file is easier to back up, version, and deploy (e.g., embedded in a mobile app).
  - **Cost**: No additional dependencies or infrastructure.

- **Cons:**
  - **Limited scalability**: SQLite’s full-text search and vector search (via extensions like `sqlite-vec`) may degrade with >100k embeddings or high-dimensional vectors (>512 dims).
  - **Less optimized**: Vector search in SQLite is not as tuned as dedicated vector DBs (e.g., no native HNSW, limited GPU support).
  - **Feature gaps**: Harder to add advanced vector operations (e.g., dynamic filtering, hybrid search).

- **When justified?**
  - **Small, single-user scope**: Ideal for your case (30k memories) if performance is acceptable and simplicity is prioritized.
  - **Embedded/local use**: No network or distributed requirements.
  - **Prototyping/early stages**: Lower risk of sync bugs and easier to iterate.

---

### **Recommendation for Your Case (30k Memories)**
- **Default to sqlite-vec** unless you profile and hit bottlenecks. The dual-store complexity isn’t justified for this scale unless you anticipate:
  - Embeddings >512 dimensions or needing advanced indexing.
  - Future multi-user/distributed needs.
  - Critical performance requirements (benchmark both first).
- **If dual-store is chosen**:
  - Use **transactions** to sync writes (e.g., insert/delete in both or none).
  - Add **idempotency checks** (e.g., ignore duplicate updates).
  - Monitor for **drift** (e.g., periodic consistency checks).

---
### **Mitigation for Dual-Store Risk**
To reduce sync risk:
1. **Atomic operations**: Wrap SQLite + vector DB updates in a single transaction (if the vector DB supports it) or use a queue with acknowledgments.
2. **Eventual consistency**: Design for graceful degradation (e.g., prefer SQLite metadata over stale vectors).
3. **Validation layer**: Before retrieval, verify the vector exists in the DB and matches the metadata.

## review-20260924T100941-0035 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-5

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

A python asyncio call to a thread-pool executor never times out even though it's wrapped in asyncio.wait_for. Why doesn't the timeout fire, and what's the correct fix?

### Response

The issue you're describing—where `asyncio.wait_for()` doesn't trigger a timeout for a thread-pool executor (`loop.run_in_executor()`)—is a common pitfall in `asyncio`. Here are the most likely root causes and fixes:

---

### **Root Cause 1: The Thread-Pool Task is Blocking Indefinitely**
- **Why it happens**:
  - If the function passed to `run_in_executor()` is stuck in an infinite loop, blocking I/O, or waiting on a non-timeoutable operation (e.g., a blocking `time.sleep()` or a deadlock), `wait_for()` will never raise `TimeoutError`.
  - `wait_for()` only times out if the coroutine itself yields control to the event loop (e.g., via `await`). Thread-pool tasks don't yield to the event loop—they run in a separate thread and only return when the function completes.

- **Fix**:
  - Ensure the function in the thread pool has a timeout mechanism (e.g., use `signal.alarm()` or a library like `concurrent.futures.TimeoutError`).
  - Example:
    ```python
    from concurrent.futures import TimeoutError as FuturesTimeoutError
    import signal

    def blocking_function_with_timeout():
        try:
            signal.alarm(5)  # Set a 5-second timeout (Unix-only)
            # Your blocking code here
        except TimeoutError:
            raise TimeoutError("Thread pool task timed out")
        finally:
            signal.alarm(0)  # Cancel the alarm

    async def async_wrapper():
        try:
            return await loop.run_in_executor(executor, blocking_function_with_timeout)
        except TimeoutError:
            print("Timeout occurred in thread pool!")
    ```

---

### **Root Cause 2: The Thread Pool is Overloaded or Deadlocked**
- **Why it happens**:
  - If the thread pool is exhausted (all threads are busy) or deadlocked, the task may never start or complete. `wait_for()` won't time out because the task isn't running.
  - Check for thread leaks or excessive thread usage.

- **Fix**:
  - Limit the thread pool size and monitor for deadlocks:
    ```python
    from concurrent.futures import ThreadPoolExecutor

    executor = ThreadPoolExecutor(max_workers=4)  # Limit workers
    ```
  - Use `executor.shutdown(wait=True)` to clean up threads if needed.

---

### **Root Cause 3: `wait_for()` is Not Wrapping the Correct Coroutine**
- **Why it happens**:
  - If you mistakenly wrap a non-awaitable (e.g., a blocking call directly) in `wait_for()`, it won't work:
    ```python
    # WRONG: This won't timeout!
    asyncio.wait_for(blocking_function(), timeout=1.0)  # blocking_function() is not a coroutine
    ```
  - `wait_for()` only works with coroutines or `Future`s.

- **Fix**:
  - Always wrap `run_in_executor()` in `wait_for()`:
    ```python
    # CORRECT:
    try:
        result = await asyncio.wait_for(
            loop.run_in_executor(executor, blocking_function),
            timeout=1.0
        )
    except asyncio.TimeoutError:
        print("Thread pool task timed out!")
    ```

---

### **Root Cause 4: The Event Loop is Busy or Starved**
- **Why it happens**:
  - If the event loop is blocked (e.g., by another long-running task), `wait_for()` may not schedule the timeout callback in time.
  - This is rare but possible in complex async code.

- **Fix**:
  - Ensure the event loop isn't starved. Use `loop.call_soon()` or `loop.call_later()` for critical timeouts.

---

### **Best Practice: Use `as_completed` or `shield` for Robust Timeouts**
For thread-pool tasks, consider:
1. **`as_completed` with a timeout**:
   ```python
   from asyncio import as_completed

   futures = [loop.run_in_executor(executor, task) for task in tasks]
   for future in as_completed(futures, timeout=1.0):
       try:
           result = future.result()
       except TimeoutError:
           print("Task timed out!")
   ```
2. **`shield` to prevent exceptions from crashing the event loop**:
   ```python
   try:
       result = await asyncio.shield(
           asyncio.wait_for(
               loop.run_in_executor(executor, blocking_function),
               timeout=1.0
           )
       )
   except TimeoutError:
       print("Timeout!")
   ```

---

### **Key Takeaway**
- **`wait_for()` only times out if the coroutine yields control to the event loop**. Thread-pool tasks don't yield, so you must enforce timeouts inside the blocking function (e.g., with `signal.alarm` or `concurrent.futures.TimeoutError`).
- Always wrap `run_in_executor()` in `wait_for()` and handle `TimeoutError` explicitly.

### Task

If a memory pipeline switched its embedding model but did NOT re-embed the existing corpus, what specifically would degrade, and why would search appear to 'mostly work' while quietly getting worse?

### Response

**Degradation:**
1. **Semantic Relevance** – Existing embeddings no longer align with the new model’s vector space, causing mismatches between queries and documents.
2. **Retrieval Quality** – Precision/recall drops for nuanced or domain-specific queries, as the new model may encode semantics differently.

**Why it "mostly works":**
- **High-frequency/broad terms** (e.g., "cat," "Python") may still map reasonably well across models, preserving *some* relevance.
- **Short queries** or exact matches rely less on semantic nuance, masking degradation.
- **User tolerance** for minor drift in results may go unnoticed unless explicitly tested.

**Silent failure modes:**
- **Long-tail queries** (e.g., "quantum error correction in superconductors") fail catastrophically.
- **Gradual drift** in rankings over time as the corpus ages without re-embedding.
- **Metrics like MRR/NDCG** degrade subtly but consistently in controlled evaluations.

## review-20260924T100941-0041 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-11

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

Explain Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) for combining keyword and vector search results to a competent developer who has never used it. Include the formula and why the constant k matters, in under 120 words.

### Response

**Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF)** combines ranked lists (e.g., keyword and vector search) by fusing their reciprocal ranks. For each result, compute:

\[
\text{RRF}(r_1, r_2, \dots, r_n) = \left( \sum_{i=1}^n \frac{1}{r_i + k} \right)^{-1}
\]

- **\(r_i\)**: Rank of the result in the *i-th* list (1 = top result).
- **\(k\)**: A constant (typically **60**) to dampen the influence of lower-ranked results.

**Why \(k\) matters**: Without it, results ranked poorly in one list (e.g., \(r_i = 100\)) would dominate the fusion. \(k\) balances contributions, preventing extreme ranks from skewing the score. Larger \(k\) favors consensus (higher ranks in *all* lists).

## review-20260924T100941-0043 - Candidate A - code_gen

Case: code-1

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, idiomatic implementation, edge-case handling, and compliance with the requested interface.

### Task

Find the bugs in this Python function:

def retry_with_backoff(func, max_retries=3, base_delay=1.0):
    for attempt in range(max_retries):
        try:
            return func()
        except Exception as e:
            if attempt == max_retries:
                raise
            delay = base_delay * (2 ** attempt)
            time.sleep(delay)


### Response

Here are the bugs in the provided code:

1. **Incorrect `attempt` condition in `if` statement**:
   The condition `if attempt == max_retries` should be `if attempt == max_retries - 1` (or better, `if attempt == max_retries: raise` moved *after* the loop).

2. **`time` module not imported**:
   The code uses `time.sleep()` but does not import the `time` module.

3. **No handling of non-exception cases**:
   If `func()` succeeds on the first attempt, the loop exits immediately, but the `except` block is still reachable (though logically unreachable). This is not a bug per se, but the logic could be clearer with a `finally` block or restructuring.

4. **No type hints**:
   The function lacks type hints, which could lead to runtime issues or make the code harder to maintain.

5. **No logging or feedback**:
   The function silently retries without logging attempts or delays, which could make debugging harder.

6. **Potential infinite loop if `max_retries=0`**:
   If `max_retries=0`, the loop never runs, and the function immediately raises any exception without retrying. This might not be the intended behavior.

7. **No handling of `KeyboardInterrupt` or other interruptible exceptions**:
   The function does not handle cases where the user interrupts execution (e.g., `KeyboardInterrupt`).

### Suggested Fixes:
```python
import time
from typing import Callable, Any

def retry_with_backoff(func: Callable[[], Any], max_retries: int = 3, base_delay: float = 1.0) -> Any:
    for attempt in range(max_retries):
        try:
            return func()
        except Exception as e:
            if attempt == max_retries - 1:  # Last attempt failed
                raise
            delay = base_delay * (2 ** attempt)
            time.sleep(delay)
```

### Response

```python
def parse_rank(text: str) -> int:
    for char in text:
        if char.isdigit() and 1 <= int(char) <= 5:
            return int(char)
    return 3
```

### Task

Write `clamp_unit(x: float) -> float | None` that clamps to [0,1], normalizes a value >1 and <=10 by /10, >10 and <=100 by /100, returns None for >100 or NaN or <0 after normalization. Include one pytest test covering the normalization and the None cases.

### Response

```python
import math

def clamp_unit(x: float) -> float | None:
    if math.isnan(x) or x < 0:
        return None
    if x <= 1:
        return x
    if x <= 10:
        return x / 10
    if x <= 100:
        return x / 100
    return None

import pytest

def test_clamp_unit():
    assert clamp_unit(0.5) == 0.5
    assert clamp_unit(15) == 1.5
    assert clamp_unit(105) == 1.05
    assert clamp_unit(101) is None
    assert clamp_unit(-1) is None
    assert clamp_unit(float('nan')) is None
```

What does this do, and what's the subtle correctness problem?

seen = set()
def dedupe(items):
    out = []
    for it in items:
        if it not in seen:
            seen.add(it)
            out.append(it)
    return out


### Response

**Purpose:** Removes duplicates from `items` while preserving order.

**Subtle Problem:** Uses a **set** (`seen`) for O(1) lookups, but if `items` contains **unhashable types** (e.g., lists, dicts), it raises `TypeError`. Example:
```python
dedupe([1, [2], 3])  # Fails: unhashable type: 'list'
```

Case: session-1

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for accurate synthesis, useful reflection, coverage of what worked and failed, and absence of invented events. Do not reward verbosity.

### Task

Summary: Debugged terrible audio from a MAX98357 on an ESP32; root cause was an impedance mismatch and a loose JST connector. Switched to JST-PH connectors and a matched 4-ohm speaker, which fixed the noise.

User: the MAX98357 on my esp32 sounds like garbage, mostly noise.
Assistant: That's often impedance mismatch or loose wiring. What speaker ohms?
User: not sure, and the connectors are dupont and pretty loose.
Assistant: Dupont wires on audio are unreliable — switch to JST-PH 2-pin, and confirm the speaker is 4ohm to match the MAX98357. A wrong-impedance speaker also distorts.
User: swapped to JST-PH and a 4ohm speaker — clean now. the speaker was 8ohm before.
Assistant: Good — the 8ohm mismatch plus loose contacts explains the noise.

### Response

EFFECTIVENESS: effective

WHAT_WORKED:
- Identifying impedance mismatch (8ohm vs. MAX98357’s 4ohm requirement) as the primary distortion source.
- Pinpointing loose JST connectors (Dupont) as a secondary noise contributor.
- Directing the user to a specific connector type (JST-PH) and impedance-matched component (4ohm speaker).

WHAT_DIDNT_WORK:
- No wasted effort; all hypotheses led to immediate resolution.

TECHNICAL_INSIGHTS:
- MAX98357 expects a 4ohm load; higher-impedance speakers (e.g., 8ohm) cause distortion and noise.
- Dupont connectors introduce unreliable contact for audio signals due to springiness and misalignment.
- JST-PH connectors provide stable, low-resistance connections for audio applications.
- Loose connections on audio lines manifest as white noise or crackling, not just signal loss.

PROCESS_INSIGHTS:
- For audio issues, verify both impedance matching and physical connections before assuming component failure.
- When troubleshooting analog noise, prioritize signal path integrity (connectors, traces) alongside component specs.
- Component datasheets often specify load impedance; cross-check against actual speaker ratings.

Case: session-2

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for accurate synthesis, useful reflection, coverage of what worked and failed, and absence of invented events. Do not reward verbosity.

### Task

Summary: Diagnosed a duplicate-message bug: assistant messages were saved twice because _persist_message was fire-and-forget, so _memory_db_ids was empty when the enqueue ran. Fixed by tracking persist tasks and awaiting them before enqueue.

User: every assistant message is getting saved twice in the DB.
Assistant: Sounds like a race. Let me check how messages persist.
Assistant: Found it — _persist_message uses asyncio.ensure_future (fire-and-forget), so _memory_db_ids isn't populated when _enqueue_undumped_messages runs. The processor gets memory_id=None and inserts a duplicate row.
User: how do we fix it cleanly?
Assistant: Track persist tasks in a list and await them in flush_pending_persists() before the enqueue reads _memory_db_ids. Make _enqueue_undumped_messages async and await the flush first.

### Response

EFFECTIVENESS: effective

WHAT_WORKED:
- Identified the root cause as a race condition between fire-and-forget async tasks (_persist_message) and the subsequent read of _memory_db_ids in _enqueue_undumped_messages.
- Proposed a concrete fix: tracking persist tasks in a list and awaiting them in a dedicated flush method before enqueueing.
- Recognized the need to make _enqueue_undumped_messages async to properly await the flush operation.

WHAT_DIDNT_WORK:
- No dead ends or wasted effort were evident; the diagnosis and fix were direct and efficient.

TECHNICAL_INSIGHTS:
- Fire-and-forget async tasks (via asyncio.ensure_future) can lead to race conditions when subsequent operations depend on their side effects.
- Tracking async tasks in a list allows for explicit control over their completion order.
- Awaiting task completion in a dedicated flush method ensures data consistency before dependent operations proceed.
- Making a function async and awaiting its dependencies requires careful refactoring of call sites to propagate async/await correctly.

PROCESS_INSIGHTS:
- Race conditions in async code often stem from assumptions about task completion order; explicit tracking and awaiting can resolve them.
- When debugging async race conditions, focus on the timing and dependencies of shared state (e.g., _memory_db_ids) rather than just the task definitions.
- Refactoring to await dependencies may require cascading changes to ensure all call paths support async/await.

## review-20260924T100941-0054 - Candidate A - session_review

### Task

Summary: Fixed search returning almost nothing at 31k memories: compounding filters (similarity 0.5 → rank ≥3 → score floor) removed ~85% of candidates. Replaced the rank filter with an importance floor, lowered the similarity threshold, and added a recency boost.

User: search barely returns anything now that the corpus is huge.
Assistant: Your filters compound — similarity 0.5, then rank≥3, then a score floor. On 31k memories that strips ~85% of candidates.
User: what should change?
Assistant: Drop the rank filter for a continuous importance floor (0.25), lower similarity to ~0.35, give FTS-only hits a baseline similarity, and add a recency boost.
User: recall is way better now.

### Response

EFFECTIVENESS: effective

WHAT_WORKED:
- Identifying compounding filters (similarity + rank + score floor) as the root cause of recall loss by quantifying their impact (~85% candidate reduction).
- Replacing the discrete rank filter (≥3) with a continuous importance floor (0.25) to preserve more candidates while maintaining relevance.
- Introducing a recency boost to offset the relaxed similarity threshold without sacrificing freshness.

WHAT_DIDNT_WORK:
- The original multi-stage filtering pipeline (similarity → rank → score floor) failed to scale with corpus size due to aggressive thresholds.
- No explicit fallback mechanism for FTS-only results, which could have been handled earlier to avoid recall drops.

TECHNICAL_INSIGHTS:
- Compound filters in vector search amplify candidate elimination exponentially; each stage’s selectivity multiplies the reduction rate.
- Discrete rank thresholds (e.g., ≥3) are brittle for large corpora; continuous floors (e.g., importance ≥0.25) allow finer control.
- Recency can be injected as a multiplicative boost (e.g., `score *= 1 + recency_weight`) without altering the underlying similarity metric.
- FTS (full-text search) results should have a baseline similarity floor to avoid being entirely excluded from hybrid ranking.

PROCESS_INSIGHTS:
- Diagnose recall issues by isolating and measuring the impact of each filter stage, not just the final output.
- Replace rigid thresholds with tunable floors or weights when dealing with dynamic corpus sizes.
- Test recency and importance boosts incrementally to balance freshness and relevance without overfitting.

TECHNICAL_INSIGHTS:
- sqlite-vec’s vec0 tables allow vector storage in the same SQLite DB file, avoiding external sync.
- Chroma’s prebuilt index is not directly portable to sqlite-vec, requiring full re-embedding.
- FK errors in dual-store setups stem from eventual consistency between Chroma and SQLite.

PROCESS_INSIGHTS:

- Consolidation (single-store) is a viable fix for synchronization problems in vector databases.

## review-20260924T100941-0091 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-2

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

Why might an ESP32 with a MAX98357 I2S DAC produce noise or garbage audio? List the 3 most likely causes and how to debug each one.

### Response

1. **Incorrect I2S Configuration**
   - **Cause:** Wrong bit depth, sample rate, or clock settings (e.g., mismatched with DAC specs).
   - **Debug:**
     - Verify DAC datasheet settings (MAX98357: **16-bit, 44.1kHz–96kHz**, standard I2S mode).
     - Check ESP32 I2S driver config (`i2s_config_t`):
       ```cpp
       i2s_config_t i2s_config = {
           .mode = (i2s_mode_t)(INIT_MODE_I2S_MASTER | I2S_MODE_TX),
           .sample_rate = 44100,
           .bits_per_sample = I2S_BITS_PER_SAMPLE_16BIT,
           .channel_format = I2S_CHANNEL_FMT_RIGHT_LEFT,
           .communication_format = I2S_COMM_FORMAT_STAND_I2S,
           .intr_alloc_flags = ESP_INTR_FLAG_LEVEL1,
           .dma_buf_count = 8,
           .dma_buf_len = 1024,
           .use_apll = false,
           .tx_desc_auto_clear = true
       };
       ```
     - Use an oscilloscope/logic analyzer to confirm I2S signals (WS, SCK, SD).

---

2. **Power Supply Issues**
   - **Cause:** DAC requires stable **3.3V (VCC)** and **quiet ground** (no noise coupling).
   - **Debug:**
     - Measure **VCC ripple** (should be <50mV). Use a low-ESR capacitor (e.g., 10µF + 0.1µF) near DAC.
     - Check for ground loops; use a **star ground** for DAC, ESP32, and audio shield.
     - Test with a **linear regulator** (e.g., LD1117V33) if power is noisy.

---

3. **Clock Instability or Jitter**
   - **Cause:** ESP32’s default APLL or internal clock may introduce jitter, degrading audio quality.
   - **Debug:**
     - **Disable APLL** (set `use_apll = false` in config) and use ESP32’s internal 80MHz clock.
     - **Oversample** (if supported) to reduce jitter effects (e.g., 48kHz instead of 44.1kHz).
     - Test with a **crystal oscillator** (e.g., 40MHz) for the ESP32 if jitter persists.
     - Use a **low-jitter buffer** (e.g., double-buffering in code) to smooth data transitions.

## review-20260924T100941-0092 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-3

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

How do I connect an SSD1306 OLED to an ESP32?

### Response

Here’s the improved version:

---
To connect an **SSD1306 OLED** to an **ESP32**, follow these wiring steps:

1. **Power & Ground**:
   - **VCC** → **3.3V** (ESP32’s 3.3V pin)
   - **GND** → **GND** (ESP32’s ground)

2. **I²C Pins** (most common configuration):
   - **SDA** → **GPIO 21** (default I²C SDA on many ESP32 boards)
   - **SCL** → **GPIO 22** (default I²C SCL on many ESP32 boards)

   *(Note: Some ESP32 boards may use **GPIO 4 (SDA)** and **GPIO 5 (SCL)**—verify your board’s pinout.)*

3. **Library Setup**:
   - Install the **Adafruit SSD1306** and **Adafruit GFX** libraries via Arduino Library Manager.
   - Include the library:
     ```cpp
     #include <Wire.h>
     #include <Adafruit_GFX.h>
     #include <Adafruit_SSD1306.h>
     ```

4. **Initialization** (adjust I²C address if needed):
   ```cpp
   #define SCREEN_WIDTH 128
   #define SCREEN_HEIGHT 64
   #define OLED_RESET    -1

   Adafruit_SSD1306 display(SCREEN_WIDTH, SCREEN_HEIGHT, &Wire, OLED_RESET);

   void setup() {
     Wire.begin(21, 22); // SDA, SCL (adjust if using different pins)
     if(!display.begin(SSD1306_SWITCHCAPVCC, 0x3C)) { // Try 0x3D if 0x3C fails
       Serial.println(F("SSD1306 allocation failed"));
     }
   }
   ```

**Key Notes**:
- **I²C Address**: Most SSD1306 displays use `0x3C` or `0x3D` (check your datasheet).
- **Alternative Pins**: If using non-default I²C pins, modify `Wire.begin()` and connections.
- **Power**: Ensure stable 3.3V (some displays need a level shifter if using 5V logic).

## review-20260924T100941-0093 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-4

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

For a single-user local assistant with 30k memories, compare a dual-store setup (SQLite + a separate vector DB) vs a single store (sqlite-vec inside the SQLite file). What's the main risk of the dual-store approach and when is it nonetheless justified?

### Response

### **Comparison: Dual-Store (SQLite + Vector DB) vs. Single-Store (sqlite-vec)**

#### **Dual-Store (SQLite + Vector DB)**
- **Pros:**
  - **Optimized for each use case**: SQLite handles relational metadata (e.g., user context, timestamps, metadata), while the vector DB (e.g., Milvus, Qdrant, Weaviate) excels at fast similarity search.
  - **Scalability**: Vector DBs are often designed for high-dimensional data and can scale better for large embeddings (if needed later).
  - **Flexibility**: Easier to swap out the vector DB if requirements change (e.g., switching from ANN to exact search).
  - **Isolation**: Corruption in one DB doesn’t necessarily break the other.

- **Cons:**
  - **Complexity**: Need to sync two databases (inserts, updates, deletes must be atomic across both).
  - **Consistency risk**: Dual-write logic increases chance of data drift (e.g., a memory deleted in SQLite but not in the vector DB).
  - **Overhead**: Extra I/O, network calls (if vector DB is separate), and maintenance (backups, monitoring).
  - **Cost (if cloud)**: Running a separate vector DB may add infrastructure costs.

- **Main Risk**:
  **Data inconsistency** due to failed syncs between stores. This is the biggest pragmatic concern for a single-user local app—if the assistant retrieves stale or missing memories, it breaks trust.

---

#### **Single-Store (sqlite-vec)**
- **Pros:**
  - **Simplicity**: Single write path, no sync issues. Atomic transactions ensure consistency.
  - **Performance**: Lower latency (no cross-DB queries or joins).
  - **Local-only**: No external dependencies; works offline by default.
  - **Cost**: Zero additional infrastructure.

- **Cons:**
  - **Trade-offs**: SQLite’s full-text search and vector search may not be as optimized as specialized DBs (e.g., slower for high-dimensional vectors).
  - **Less flexible**: Harder to replace the vector search backend later.
  - **Bloat**: Storing vectors in SQLite may increase file size (though 30k memories is manageable).

---

### **When is Dual-Store Justified?**
Only if you **anticipate future needs** that sqlite-vec can’t easily satisfy, such as:
1. **Scaling beyond local**: If the app might later support multi-user or cloud sync, a dedicated vector DB (e.g., Milvus, Pinecone) is easier to distribute.
2. **Advanced vector ops**: Need for dynamic embeddings, hybrid search (keyword + vector), or GPU-accelerated search that sqlite-vec doesn’t support.
3. **Specialized tooling**: Existing workflows rely on a vector DB (e.g., integrating with a ML pipeline that expects a specific vector store).

**For a single-user local assistant with 30k memories, sqlite-vec is the pragmatic choice**—simpler, more reliable, and sufficient for the scale. Dual-store adds unnecessary risk without clear upside.

## review-20260924T100941-0094 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-5

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

A python asyncio call to a thread-pool executor never times out even though it's wrapped in asyncio.wait_for. Why doesn't the timeout fire, and what's the correct fix?

### Response

The issue you're describing—where `asyncio.wait_for()` doesn't timeout when used with a **ThreadPoolExecutor**—is a known behavior in Python's `asyncio` due to how thread-pool tasks are scheduled and how timeouts are enforced. Here are the most likely root causes and fixes:

---

### **Root Cause:**
1. **ThreadPoolExecutor Tasks Are Not Properly Monitored by `asyncio`**
   - `asyncio.wait_for()` works by raising `asyncio.TimeoutError` when the coroutine doesn't complete within the given timeout.
   - However, when the coroutine submits work to a **ThreadPoolExecutor**, the timeout mechanism may not properly track the thread's execution because:
     - Threads run outside the event loop's control.
     - The `asyncio` event loop cannot directly interrupt a thread (unlike coroutines, which can be canceled via `Task.cancel()`).
   - If the thread's task is **blocking** (e.g., due to I/O, CPU-bound work, or a deadlock), `wait_for` may silently wait indefinitely.

2. **The Thread Task Is Not Actually Blocking the Event Loop**
   - If the thread task completes quickly but the `asyncio` event loop is stuck (e.g., due to a deadlock or another blocking call), the timeout may not trigger.

3. **The Timeout Is Too Long or the Thread Task Is Hanging**
   - If the thread task is stuck (e.g., in a long-running operation or a deadlock), `wait_for` will not raise a timeout.

4. **The ThreadPoolExecutor Is Misconfigured**
   - If the executor is not properly integrated with `asyncio` (e.g., using an old `loop.run_in_executor` pattern), timeouts may not work as expected.

---

### **Correct Fixes:**
#### **1. Use `loop.run_in_executor` with `asyncio.wait_for` (Recommended)**
   - Ensure the thread task is properly submitted and monitored:
     ```python
     import asyncio

     async def thread_task():
         # Simulate a long-running thread task
         await asyncio.sleep(5)  # This runs in the thread pool
         return "Done"

     async def main():
         loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
         try:
             result = await asyncio.wait_for(
                 loop.run_in_executor(
                     None,  # Uses the default ThreadPoolExecutor
                     thread_task
                 ),
                 timeout=1.0  # Timeout in seconds
             )
             print(result)
         except asyncio.TimeoutError:
             print("Thread task timed out!")

     asyncio.run(main())
     ```
   - **Key Point:** `run_in_executor` returns a `Future`, which `wait_for` can monitor.

#### **2. Ensure the Thread Task Is Not Blocking the Event Loop**
   - If the thread task is **CPU-bound**, consider using `ProcessPoolExecutor` instead (since threads in Python are limited by the GIL).
   - If the thread task is **I/O-bound**, ensure it doesn’t block indefinitely (e.g., by using timeouts in the thread itself).

#### **3. Use a Timeout in the Thread Task Itself**
   - If you control the thread task, add a timeout inside it:
     ```python
     def thread_task_with_timeout():
         try:
             # Simulate work with a timeout
             result = some_blocking_call(timeout=1.0)
             return result
         except TimeoutError:
             raise  # Re-raise to propagate to asyncio
     ```

#### **4. Check for Event Loop Deadlocks**
   - If the event loop is stuck (e.g., due to another blocking call), `wait_for` won’t work. Use:
     ```python
     async def main():
         try:
             await asyncio.wait_for(some_coroutine(), timeout=1.0)
         except asyncio.TimeoutError:
             print("Timeout!")
         except Exception as e:
             print(f"Error: {e}")
     ```

#### **5. Use `asyncio.to_thread` (Python 3.9+)**
   - If you're using Python 3.9+, `asyncio.to_thread` is a cleaner way to run blocking code in a thread:
     ```python
     async def main():
         try:
             result = await asyncio.wait_for(
                 asyncio.to_thread(blocking_function, arg1, arg2),
                 timeout=1.0
             )
         except asyncio.TimeoutError:
             print("Thread task timed out!")
     ```

---

### **Debugging Steps:**
1. **Check if the thread task is actually running:**
   - Add logging inside the thread task to confirm it’s executing.
2. **Test with a shorter timeout:**
   - If the timeout still doesn’t fire, the thread task is likely stuck.
3. **Use `ProcessPoolExecutor` for CPU-bound work:**
   - Threads won’t help with CPU-bound tasks due to the GIL.
4. **Verify the event loop is not deadlocked:**
   - Run the code in a fresh event loop to rule out deadlocks.

---

### **Example of a Working Timeout:**
```python
import asyncio

async def thread_task():
    await asyncio.sleep(2)  # Simulate work
    return "Done"

async def main():
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    try:
        result = await asyncio.wait_for(
            loop.run_in_executor(None, thread_task),
            timeout=1.0
        )
        print(result)
    except asyncio.TimeoutError:
        print("Thread task timed out!")  # This will print

asyncio.run(main())
```

---

### **Key Takeaway:**
- `asyncio.wait_for` works with `run_in_executor`/`to_thread`, but **thread tasks must not block indefinitely**.
- If the thread task hangs, the timeout won’t trigger.
- For CPU-bound work, use `ProcessPoolExecutor` instead.

### Task

If a memory pipeline switched its embedding model but did NOT re-embed the existing corpus, what specifically would degrade, and why would search appear to 'mostly work' while quietly getting worse?

### Response

1. **Degradation**:
   - **Relevance**: Existing embeddings become mismatched with the new model’s vector space → semantic relationships degrade.
   - **Consistency**: Old and new embeddings live in incompatible spaces → retrieval drift.
   - **Ranking**: Similarity scores (e.g., cosine) lose meaning → poor discrimination between relevant/irrelevant docs.

2. **"Works" but quietly worsens**:
   - **Short-term**: High-frequency/well-embedded queries (e.g., exact matches) still retrieve *some* relevant docs by chance.
   - **Long-term**: Nuanced queries (e.g., synonyms, rare terms) fail as old embeddings lack alignment with the new model’s semantics.
   - **Silent drift**: No explicit error → gradual, undetected degradation in precision/recall for edge cases.

**Why it seems fine**: Most queries hit cached or broadly similar vectors; failures are subtle (e.g., missing rare but critical docs).

## review-20260924T100941-0100 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-11

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

Explain Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) for combining keyword and vector search results to a competent developer who has never used it. Include the formula and why the constant k matters, in under 120 words.

### Response

**Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF)** combines ranked lists (e.g., keyword and vector search) by fusing their reciprocal ranks. For each document, compute:

\[
\text{RRF}(d) = \sum_{i=1}^{m} \frac{1}{r_{i,d} + k}
\]

- **\(r_{i,d}\)**: Rank of document \(d\) in list \(i\).
- **\(k\)**: A constant (typically **60**) to dampen high ranks and prevent dominance by a single list.

**Why \(k\) matters**: A small \(k\) favors top ranks heavily; a large \(k\) balances contributions. Empirically, \(k=60\) works well for most cases. Re-rank documents by descending RRF scores.

### Response

Here are the bugs in the provided `retry_with_backoff` function:

1. **Incorrect `attempt` condition in `if` statement**:
   The condition `if attempt == max_retries` should be `if attempt == max_retries - 1` (or better, `if attempt >= max_retries - 1`), because `attempt` ranges from `0` to `max_retries - 1`.

2. **Missing `import time`**:
   The function uses `time.sleep()`, but the `time` module is not imported.

3. **No handling of non-exception cases**:
   If `func()` succeeds on the first try, the loop exits immediately, which is correct, but the logic could be clearer with an early `return` outside the loop (though this is not technically a bug).

4. **No type hints**:
   The function lacks type hints (e.g., `Callable`, `float`, `int`), which could help catch issues early.

5. **No logging or warning for retries**:
   While not strictly a bug, it could be useful to log or warn when retries are attempted (e.g., `print(f"Retry {attempt + 1}/{max_retries}...")`).

6. **No handling of `KeyboardInterrupt` or other interruptible exceptions**:
   The function will silently retry on `KeyboardInterrupt`, which may not be desired.

7. **Potential infinite loop if `max_retries=0`**:
   If `max_retries=0`, the loop never runs, and the function immediately raises any exception from `func()` without a retry. This might be intentional, but it’s worth documenting.

8. **No handling of `StopIteration` or other edge cases**:
   The function catches all exceptions (`Exception`), which might mask unexpected behavior in some cases.

### Fixed version (addressing critical issues):
```python
import time
from typing import Callable, Any

def retry_with_backoff(func: Callable[[], Any], max_retries: int = 3, base_delay: float = 1.0) -> Any:
    for attempt in range(max_retries):
        try:
            return func()
        except Exception as e:
            if attempt == max_retries - 1:  # Fixed condition
                raise
            delay = base_delay * (2 ** attempt)
            time.sleep(delay)
```

### Task

Write a Python function `parse_rank(text: str) -> int` that extracts a rank (1-5) from LLM output text. It should find the first digit 1-5 in the text and return it, defaulting to 3 if none found.

### Response

```python
def parse_rank(text: str) -> int:
    for char in text:
        if char.isdigit() and 1 <= int(char) <= 5:
            return int(char)
    return 3
```

## review-20260924T100941-0106 - Candidate A - code_gen

Case: code-5

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, idiomatic implementation, edge-case handling, and compliance with the requested interface.

### Task

Write `clamp_unit(x: float) -> float | None` that clamps to [0,1], normalizes a value >1 and <=10 by /10, >10 and <=100 by /100, returns None for >100 or NaN or <0 after normalization. Include one pytest test covering the normalization and the None cases.

### Response

```python
import math

def clamp_unit(x: float) -> float | None:
    if math.isnan(x):
        return None
    if x < 0:
        return None
    if x <= 1:
        return x
    if x <= 10:
        return x / 10
    if x <= 100:
        return x / 100
    return None

import pytest

def test_clamp_unit():
    assert clamp_unit(0.5) == 0.5
    assert clamp_unit(15) == 0.15
    assert clamp_unit(150) == 0.15
    assert clamp_unit(105) is None
    assert clamp_unit(-1) is None
    assert math.isnan(clamp_unit(float('nan')))
```

### Task

What does this do, and what's the subtle correctness problem?

seen = set()
def dedupe(items):
    out = []
    for it in items:
        if it not in seen:
            seen.add(it)
            out.append(it)
    return out


### Response

**Purpose:** Removes duplicates from a list while preserving order.

**Subtle Correctness Problem:**
- **Hashability:** Fails if `items` contains unhashable types (e.g., lists, dicts) because `set()` and `in` operations require hashable elements.
  *Fix:* Use a list for `seen` and `if it not in seen` (slower but works for unhashable types).

### Task

This function does keyword filtering to decide which tools to pass to a model. Successful coding agents don't gate tools this way. Briefly explain why, and what to do instead.

### Response

**Why not?**
- **Overly restrictive**: Hardcoded keywords limit adaptability to nuanced or evolving use cases.
- **Brittle**: Misspellings, synonyms, or context shifts break filtering.
- **Agent autonomy**: Gates prevent the model from dynamically assessing tool relevance.

**Refactor to:**
```python
def select_tools(agent_input, available_tools):
    # Let the model evaluate tools based on:
    # 1. **Intent** (e.g., "summarize" → use `summarize_tool`)
    # 2. **Context** (e.g., "API call needed" → use `api_tool`)
    # 3. **Capabilities** (e.g., "requires math" → use `calculate_tool`)
    return model.rank_tools_by_relevance(agent_input, available_tools)[:3]  # Top 3
```

**Key shift**: Replace rigid filters with **dynamic relevance scoring** (e.g., embeddings + LLM evaluation). Tools are pre-registered; the agent decides at runtime.

User: every assistant message is getting saved twice in the DB.

Assistant: Track persist tasks in a list and await them in flush_pending_persists() before the enqueue reads _memory_db_ids. Make _enqueue_undumped_messages async and await the flush first.

### Response

EFFECTIVENESS: effective

WHAT_WORKED:
- Identified the root cause as a race condition between fire-and-forget async tasks (_persist_message) and the subsequent read of _memory_db_ids by _enqueue_undumped_messages.
- Proposed a concrete fix: tracking persist tasks in a list and awaiting them in a new `flush_pending_persists()` method before enqueueing.

WHAT_DIDNT_WORK:
- No dead ends or wasted effort were evident; the diagnosis and fix were direct and targeted.

TECHNICAL_INSIGHTS:
- Fire-and-forget async tasks (e.g., asyncio.ensure_future without awaiting) can lead to race conditions when subsequent operations depend on their side effects.
- Tracking async tasks in a list allows for explicit control over their completion order via await.
- Async methods must be properly awaited in synchronous contexts to prevent race conditions (e.g., awaiting `flush_pending_persists()` before reading _memory_db_ids).
- The _memory_db_ids state must be fully populated before any enqueue operation that relies on it.

PROCESS_INSIGHTS:
- Race conditions in async code often stem from missing awaits or improper task ordering; explicitly tracking and awaiting tasks resolves this.
- Debugging duplicate DB entries requires checking both the persistence logic and the state it relies on (e.g., _memory_db_ids).
- Refactoring to await critical async operations can simplify debugging by enforcing explicit dependencies.

Summary: Fixed search returning almost nothing at 31k memories: compounding filters (similarity 0.5 → rank ≥3 → score floor) removed ~85% of candidates. Replaced the rank filter with an importance floor, lowered the similarity threshold, and added a recency boost.

User: search barely returns anything now that the corpus is huge.
Assistant: Your filters compound — similarity 0.5, then rank≥3, then a score floor. On 31k memories that strips ~85% of candidates.
User: what should change?
Assistant: Drop the rank filter for a continuous importance floor (0.25), lower similarity to ~0.35, give FTS-only hits a baseline similarity, and add a recency boost.

### Response

EFFECTIVENESS: effective

WHAT_WORKED:
- Identifying compounding filters (similarity threshold + rank cutoff + score floor) as the root cause of recall loss in a large corpus.
- Replacing discrete rank filtering with a continuous importance floor (0.25) to preserve more candidates.
- Adding a recency boost to compensate for the relaxed similarity threshold without sacrificing relevance.

WHAT_DIDNT_WORK:
- The original multi-stage filtering pipeline (similarity → rank → score floor) failed to scale with corpus size, causing catastrophic recall drop.
- A hard rank cutoff (≥3) acted as a binary exclusion rather than a relative ranking signal.

TECHNICAL_INSIGHTS:
- Compound filters in vector search compound multiplicatively: each threshold reduces the candidate pool by a fraction, not a fixed amount.
- Importance floors (continuous) preserve more candidates than discrete rank cutoffs while maintaining relevance signals.
- Recency boosts can offset relaxed similarity thresholds without requiring manual tuning of multiple parameters.
- Full-text search (FTS)-only hits benefit from a baseline similarity assignment to avoid exclusion from the final pool.

PROCESS_INSIGHTS:
- Diagnose recall issues by isolating and quantifying the impact of each filter stage (e.g., "similarity 0.5 alone drops 20%, rank≥3 drops another 50%").
- Replace discrete ranking cutoffs with continuous floors when scaling to large datasets.
- Test recency/importance boosts as alternatives to tightening similarity thresholds.

### Response

## 1. Primary Request and Intent
The user wants to **re-run the nightly entity merge** (which previously archived 7,491 entities) but is concerned about **version-numbered entities** (e.g., `projectecho_v1`, `projectecho_v2`) being incorrectly merged. Key constraints:
- **Preserve versioned entities** (e.g., `v1`, `v2` suffixes) to avoid unintended merges.
- **Do not modify `cleanup_entities.py`** (as it performs hard deletes).
- **Enforce the ARCHIVE-never-DELETE rule** during the merge.
- **Take a backup** before running the merge.
- **Ensure proper cache invalidation** for entity names (`get_all_entity_names` cache) when writing entity rows outside the store’s methods.

---

## 2. Key Decisions Made
- **Re-run the merge** despite versioned entity risks, with safeguards in place.
- **Disable hard deletes** by adhering to the ARCHIVE-never-DELETE rule.
- **Backup `data/blipshell.db`** before execution.
- **Use dry-run first**, then apply with `--loop` flag.
- **No changes to `cleanup_entities.py`** (hard-delete logic remains untouched).
- **Cache invalidation requirement**: Any external writes to entity rows must call `_invalidate_entity_name_cache`.

---

## 3. Files and Code
- **Files examined**:
  - `blipshell/memory/entity_names.py` (shared logic for both merge paths).
  - `cleanup_entities.py` (hard-delete functionality; **not modified**).
- **Key logic**:
  - **EntityMerger** (disabled path): Previously had `entity_names.version_distinguished` guard (until 2026-08-07).
  - **Creation-time resolution** (enabled path): **No guard**, allowing versioned merges.
  - **Cosine similarity threshold**: `projectecho_v1` and `projectecho_v2` embeddings at **0.996** (above merge thresholds).
  - **Cache invalidation**: `_invalidate_entity_name_cache` must be called for external entity row writes.

---

## 4. Errors and Fixes
- **Potential error**: Versioned entities (e.g., `v1`, `v2`) could merge due to missing guard in the **enabled creation-time resolution path**.
  - **Fix**: No code changes needed; user opted to **proceed with backup and dry-run** while relying on ARCHIVE-never-DELETE rule as a safeguard.
- **Cache inconsistency risk**: External writes bypassing store methods could leave `get_all_entity_names` cache stale.
  - **Fix**: Mandate `_invalidate_entity_name_cache` calls for all external entity row modifications.

---

## 5. Problem-Solving Progress
- **Approaches tried**:
  - Verified guard existence in **EntityMerger** (disabled path) and absence in **creation-time resolution** (enabled path).
  - Confirmed cosine similarity would trigger merges without version guards.
  - Assessed backup and dry-run strategy to mitigate risks.
- **What worked**:
  - Dry-run + `--loop` approach for safe testing.
  - Backup of `blipshell.db` as a rollback option.
- **What didn’t work/risks**:
  - No guard in the **enabled merge path** (creation-time resolution) could still merge versioned entities.
  - Hard deletes in `cleanup_entities.py` are **untouchable** per user’s constraint.

## review-20260924T100941-0150 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-2

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

Why might an ESP32 with a MAX98357 I2S DAC produce noise or garbage audio? List the 3 most likely causes and how to debug each one.

### Response

1. **Incorrect I2S Configuration**
   - **Cause**: Wrong bit depth, sample rate, or clock settings (e.g., mismatched with DAC specs).
   - **Debug**:
     - Verify DAC datasheet settings (e.g., MAX98357: **16-bit, 44.1kHz, 32kHz, or 22.05kHz**).
     - Check ESP32 I2S peripheral setup (e.g., `I2S_NUM_0`, `I2S_BITS_PER_SAMPLE_16BIT`, `I2S_SAMPLE_RATE_44100`).
     - Use an oscilloscope/logic analyzer to confirm I2S signals (WS, SCK, SD).

2. **Power Supply Issues**
   - **Cause**: Unstable/noisy power (DAC requires clean **3.3V** and **AGND/DGND** separation).
   - **Debug**:
     - Use a **low-noise LDOs** (e.g., AMS1117) or linear regulator.
     - Decouple with **10µF + 0.1µF** caps near DAC’s **VDD** and **AGND**.
     - Measure voltage ripple (should be **<50mV**).

3. **Grounding/Layout Problems**
   - **Cause**: Poor grounding or long traces causing noise coupling.
   - **Debug**:
     - Ensure **AGND** (analog) and **DGND** (digital) are **star-grounded** at DAC’s GND pin.
     - Keep I2S traces **short and away** from noisy signals (e.g., PWM, motors).
     - Test with a **ferrite bead** on the DAC’s power line if RF interference is suspected.

## review-20260924T100941-0151 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-3

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

How do I connect an SSD1306 OLED to an ESP32?

### Response

Here’s the improved version:

---

To connect an **SSD1306 OLED** to an **ESP32**, follow these wiring steps:

- **VCC** → **3.3V** (ESP32)
- **GND** → **GND** (ESP32)
- **SCL** → **GPIO 22** (default I2C clock line)
- **SDA** → **GPIO 21** (default I2C data line)

### Required Setup:
1. **Install the library**:
   Use the **Adafruit SSD1306** library (install via Arduino Library Manager or manually from [Adafruit GitHub](https://github.com/adafruit/Adafruit_SSD1306)).

2. **Initialize the display**:
   In your code, include:
   ```cpp
   #include <Wire.h>
   #include <Adafruit_GFX.h>
   #include <Adafruit_SSD1306.h>

   // Define I2C address (common: 0x3C or 0x3D; check your display)
   #define OLED_ADDR 0x3D
   Adafruit_SSD1306 display(128, 64, &Wire, -1); // Width, height, I2C, reset pin (-1 if unused)

   void setup() {
     Wire.begin(21, 22); // SDA, SCL (optional if using default pins)
     if (!display.begin(SSD1306_SWITCHCAPVCC, OLED_ADDR)) {
       Serial.println(F("SSD1306 allocation failed"));
       for (;;); // Halt if initialization fails
     }
   }
   ```

### Notes:
- **I2C Address**: Verify your display’s address (common alternatives: `0x3C`).
- **Reset Pin**: If your display uses a reset pin, connect it to an unused GPIO (e.g., GPIO 4) and update the `display` constructor.
- **Power**: Ensure stable 3.3V power (use a capacitor if needed for flickering).

---

## review-20260924T100941-0152 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-4

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

For a single-user local assistant with 30k memories, compare a dual-store setup (SQLite + a separate vector DB) vs a single store (sqlite-vec inside the SQLite file). What's the main risk of the dual-store approach and when is it nonetheless justified?

### Response

### **Comparison: Dual-Store (SQLite + Vector DB) vs Single-Store (sqlite-vec)**

#### **Dual-Store (SQLite + Vector DB)**
- **Pros:**
  - **Mature vector DBs** (e.g., Milvus, Weaviate, Qdrant) optimize for vector search, indexing, and scalability.
  - **Flexibility**: Can swap vector DBs without changing the relational layer.
  - **Specialized optimizations**: Better handling of high-dimensional vectors, approximate nearest neighbor (ANN) search, and sharding.
  - **Isolation**: Vector DB can scale independently (e.g., if vector ops become a bottleneck).

- **Cons:**
  - **Consistency risk**: *Main risk* → **Eventual consistency** between SQLite (metadata, text) and the vector DB (embeddings). If writes fail in one but not the other, data diverges.
  - **Complexity**: Need to manage sync (e.g., transactions spanning both stores), error handling, and recovery.
  - **Overhead**: Cross-store joins (e.g., fetching metadata for a retrieved vector) add latency.
  - **Cost**: Dual storage increases disk/CPU usage and may require more resources for sync.

- **When justified?**
  - **Vector-heavy workloads**: If >80% of queries are vector-based (e.g., semantic search), a dedicated vector DB may outperform sqlite-vec.
  - **Future scalability**: If you anticipate needing to replace the vector DB later (e.g., for distributed search).
  - **Specialized features**: Need advanced vector ops (e.g., dynamic filtering, hybrid search) not yet in sqlite-vec.

---

#### **Single-Store (sqlite-vec)**
- **Pros:**
  - **ACID guarantees**: Atomicity ensures no divergence between metadata and vectors.
  - **Simplicity**: Single write path, no sync overhead, easier backups/recovery.
  - **Lower latency**: No cross-store joins; all data co-located.
  - **Resource efficiency**: Shared storage, no duplication.

- **Cons:**
  - **Limited vector scalability**: sqlite-vec may lag in performance for large-scale ANN search (though improving).
  - **Less flexibility**: Harder to swap out the vector layer later.
  - **Feature gaps**: May lack advanced vector DB features (e.g., dynamic indexing).

- **When justified?**
  - **Default choice**: For most single-user local assistants with <30k memories, sqlite-vec is sufficient and simpler.
  - **Consistency-critical**: If data integrity is paramount (e.g., financial or medical assistants).
  - **Low vector complexity**: If queries are simple (e.g., exact-match or small-scale semantic search).

---

### **Key Risk of Dual-Store: Eventual Consistency**
- **Failure modes**:
  - Partial writes (e.g., vector stored but metadata missing, or vice versa).
  - Network/IO errors during sync (e.g., vector DB crashes mid-write).
  - Stale reads (querying SQLite for metadata while the vector DB hasn’t synced).
- **Mitigations** (if dual-store is chosen):
  - Use **transactions spanning both stores** (e.g., SQLite WAL mode + vector DB transactions).
  - Implement **idempotent writes** and retries.
  - Add **checksums/validation** to detect divergence.
  - **Local-only**: For single-user, sync is trivial (no network), but you still need to handle crashes.

---
### **Recommendation**
- **Start with sqlite-vec** (single-store). It’s simpler, consistent, and good enough for 30k memories in a local assistant.
- **Switch to dual-store only if**:
  - You profile vector search as a bottleneck *and* sqlite-vec can’t scale it.
  - You need advanced vector features not in sqlite-vec.
  - You’re willing to manage the complexity of sync and consistency.

## review-20260924T100941-0153 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-5

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

A python asyncio call to a thread-pool executor never times out even though it's wrapped in asyncio.wait_for. Why doesn't the timeout fire, and what's the correct fix?

### Response

The issue you're describing—where `asyncio.wait_for()` doesn't timeout when calling a function via a `ThreadPoolExecutor`—is a known behavior in Python's `asyncio` due to how thread-pool tasks are handled internally. Here's the root cause and the correct fix:

---

### **Root Cause:**
1. **`wait_for` only cancels coroutines, not threads**
   - `asyncio.wait_for()` works by scheduling a cancellation for the coroutine after the timeout. However, when the coroutine submits work to a `ThreadPoolExecutor` (e.g., via `loop.run_in_executor`), the cancellation is **not propagated to the thread**.
   - Threads in the executor run independently of the event loop's cancellation mechanism. The thread continues executing the task even if the coroutine is canceled.

2. **No direct thread cancellation in Python**
   - Python's `threading` module does not support cooperative cancellation (like coroutines). Threads cannot be forcibly stopped; they must exit voluntarily (e.g., via `raise TimeoutError` or similar).

3. **`run_in_executor` ignores cancellation**
   - The `ThreadPoolExecutor` does not check for coroutine cancellation when executing tasks. The thread runs the task to completion unless the task itself raises an exception or checks for cancellation (which is rare).

---

### **Why It Happens in Your Case:**
If you have code like this:
```python
import asyncio

async def long_running_task():
    await asyncio.sleep(10)  # Simulate work

async def main():
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    try:
        await asyncio.wait_for(
            loop.run_in_executor(None, long_running_task),
            timeout=1.0
        )
    except asyncio.TimeoutError:
        print("Timeout fired!")  # This will NOT execute
    else:
        print("Task completed")

asyncio.run(main())
```
The `TimeoutError` will **never** be raised because:
- `run_in_executor` submits `long_running_task` to a thread, which runs independently.
- The coroutine wrapping `run_in_executor` is canceled, but the thread ignores this.

---

### **Correct Fixes:**
#### **Option 1: Raise `TimeoutError` in the Thread Itself**
Modify the task to check for cancellation (using a shared flag or `concurrent.futures.Future`):
```python
import asyncio
from concurrent.futures import TimeoutError as ThreadTimeoutError

def long_running_task_with_timeout():
    try:
        asyncio.sleep(10)  # Simulate work
    except asyncio.CancelledError:
        raise ThreadTimeoutError("Thread task timed out")

async def main():
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    try:
        await asyncio.wait_for(
            loop.run_in_executor(None, long_running_task_with_timeout),
            timeout=1.0
        )
    except asyncio.TimeoutError:
        print("Timeout fired!")  # This will now work
    except ThreadTimeoutError:
        print("Thread task explicitly timed out")

asyncio.run(main())
```
**Limitation**: This requires modifying the task to handle cancellation, which may not always be possible.

---

#### **Option 2: Use a Separate Cancellation Flag (Recommended)**
Use a `threading.Event` or `asyncio.Event` to signal the thread to exit:
```python
import asyncio
import threading

def long_running_task_with_flag(stop_event):
    while not stop_event.is_set():
        asyncio.sleep(0.1)  # Simulate work
    raise TimeoutError("Thread task timed out")

async def main():
    stop_event = threading.Event()
    loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()
    try:
        await asyncio.wait_for(
            loop.run_in_executor(
                None,
                lambda: long_running_task_with_flag(stop_event)
            ),
            timeout=1.0
        )
    except asyncio.TimeoutError:
        stop_event.set()  # Signal the thread to exit
        print("Timeout fired!")
    else:
        print("Task completed")

asyncio.run(main())
```
**Pros**:
- No need to modify the original task.
- Works reliably for thread-based timeouts.

---

#### **Option 3: Use `asyncio.to_thread` (Python 3.9+)**
If you're using Python 3.9+, `asyncio.to_thread` is designed to handle cancellation better (though it still doesn't guarantee thread interruption):
```python
async def main():
    try:
        await asyncio.wait_for(
            asyncio.to_thread(long_running_task),
            timeout=1.0
        )
    except asyncio.TimeoutError:
        print("Timeout fired!")  # May or may not work; threads are still not forcibly stopped.
    else:
        print("Task completed")
```
**Note**: This is still not a perfect solution, as threads cannot be forcibly terminated.

---

#### **Option 4: Use a Process-Based Executor (Forced Termination)**
If you can use processes instead of threads, `ProcessPoolExecutor` allows killing processes:
```python
from concurrent.futures import ProcessPoolExecutor

async def main():
    with ProcessPoolExecutor() as pool:
        try:
            await asyncio.wait_for(
                loop.run_in_executor(pool, long_running_task),
                timeout=1.0
            )
        except asyncio.TimeoutError:
            print("Timeout fired!")  # Process can be killed if needed.
        else:
            print("Task completed")
```
**Pros**:
- Processes can be terminated forcibly (though Python's `multiprocessing` is heavyweight).
**Cons**:
- Overhead of process creation.

---

### **Best Practice:**
- **For most cases**, **Option 2 (cancellation flag)** is the most reliable and portable solution.
- If you control the task code, **Option 1 (explicit `TimeoutError`)** is cleaner.
- Avoid relying on `wait_for` with threads for strict timeouts; design tasks to cooperate with cancellation.

---

### **Key Takeaway:**
`asyncio.wait_for` **cannot** timeout threads directly because Python threads cannot be canceled cooperatively. You must explicitly handle timeouts in the thread's code or use a shared flag to signal termination.

### Task

I have limited GPU. Rank these background jobs by how much they need a large local model vs a small one: memory ranking (1-5), summarization, entity extraction, importance scoring, contradiction detection. Justify each.

### Response

1. **Summarization** – Requires large models for capturing nuanced context and generating coherent, high-quality outputs.
2. **Contradiction Detection** – Needs robust contextual understanding (e.g., cross-sentence reasoning) often handled better by larger models.
3. **Importance Scoring** – Benefits from broader knowledge and deeper feature extraction, though smaller models can approximate with trade-offs.
4. **Entity Extraction** – Typically lightweight (token-level classification), but high-precision tasks (e.g., nested entities) favor larger models.
5. **Memory Ranking** – Mostly sequence- or span-based (e.g., attention over short windows), so smaller models suffice unless handling long-range dependencies.

## review-20260924T100941-0158 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-10

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

If a memory pipeline switched its embedding model but did NOT re-embed the existing corpus, what specifically would degrade, and why would search appear to 'mostly work' while quietly getting worse?

### Response

**Degradation:**
1. **Retrieval Quality** – Existing embeddings become mismatched with the new model’s vector space, reducing semantic alignment.
2. **Ranking Accuracy** – Similarity scores (e.g., cosine similarity) between old embeddings and new query embeddings lose relevance, prioritizing less relevant results.
3. **Diversity & Coverage** – The corpus’s latent distribution shifts, potentially excluding niche or semantically distant but valid matches.

**Why Search Appears to "Work":**
- **Short-Term Stability** – High-frequency, well-represented queries (e.g., common terms) may still retrieve *some* relevant results due to overlapping semantics or residual signal in the old embeddings.
- **Noise Masking** – If the new model retains *some* alignment with the old (e.g., similar architecture or pre-training), degradation is gradual rather than catastrophic.
- **User Tolerance** – Minor drops in precision/recall may go unnoticed if the system still returns *some* correct answers or if users overlook subtle quality shifts.

**Key Mechanism:**
The pipeline relies on **embedding similarity** for retrieval. Mismatched embeddings corrupt this similarity signal, but the system lacks a ground-truth signal to expose the flaw—it *appears* functional while silently degrading.

## review-20260924T100941-0159 - Candidate A - reasoning

Case: reasoning-11

Rubric: Score 0.0-1.0 for correctness, completeness, calibrated uncertainty, and actionability. Do not reward length by itself.

### Task

Explain Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) for combining keyword and vector search results to a competent developer who has never used it. Include the formula and why the constant k matters, in under 120 words.

### Response

**Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF)** combines ranked lists (e.g., keyword and vector search) by fusing their reciprocal ranks. For each document, compute:

\[
\text{RRF}(d) = \sum_{i=1}^{m} \frac{1}{\text{rank}_i(d) + k}
\]

- **`rank_i(d)`**: Document’s rank in the *i-th* search method.
- **`k`**: A constant (typically **60**) to dampen the impact of low ranks. Without it, top results dominate excessively.

**Why `k` matters**: A higher `k` reduces the weight of poorly ranked documents, preventing noise from dominating. Empirically, `k=60` balances precision and recall across diverse search methods.

What's the default I2C address of most SSD1306 OLED displays?

### Response

The default I2C address for most **128x64 and 128x32** SSD1306 OLED displays is **0x3C** or **0x3D**, depending on the manufacturer and wiring configuration (SA0 pin logic level).

For **96x16** SSD1306 displays, the default address is typically **0x3C** (SA0 = LOW) or **0x3D** (SA0 = HIGH).

Always verify with the datasheet for specific models.

### Task

Find the bugs in this Python function:

def retry_with_backoff(func, max_retries=3, base_delay=1.0):
    for attempt in range(max_retries):
        try:
            return func()
        except Exception as e:
            if attempt == max_retries:
                raise
            delay = base_delay * (2 ** attempt)
            time.sleep(delay)


### Response

Here are the bugs in the provided code:

1. **Incorrect `attempt` condition in `if` statement**:
   The condition `if attempt == max_retries` will never be true because `attempt` ranges from `0` to `max_retries - 1` (due to `range(max_retries)`). This causes the function to never raise the exception after retries are exhausted.

2. **Missing `import time`**:
   The `time.sleep()` function is used but not imported.

3. **No handling of non-exception cases**:
   If `func()` succeeds on the first attempt, the loop exits immediately, which is correct, but there’s no explicit handling for cases where `func()` might return a value that should be re-raised or handled differently.

4. **No type hints**:
   The function lacks type hints, which could lead to runtime issues or make the code harder to maintain.

5. **No logging or feedback**:
   The function silently retries without logging or notifying the caller about retries, which could be useful for debugging.

6. **Potential infinite loop if `max_retries` is 0**:
   If `max_retries=0`, the loop will never execute, but this is likely unintended behavior. The function should either enforce `max_retries >= 1` or handle this edge case explicitly.

### Corrected condition:
The `if` statement should be:
```python
if attempt == max_retries - 1:
    raise
```

### Response

```python
def parse_rank(text: str) -> int:
    for char in text:
        if char.isdigit() and 1 <= int(char) <= 5:
            return int(char)
    return 3
```

### Task

Write `clamp_unit(x: float) -> float | None` that clamps to [0,1], normalizes a value >1 and <=10 by /10, >10 and <=100 by /100, returns None for >100 or NaN or <0 after normalization. Include one pytest test covering the normalization and the None cases.

### Response

```python
import math

def clamp_unit(x: float) -> float | None:
    if math.isnan(x):
        return None
    if x < 0:
        return None
    if x <= 1:
        return x
    if x <= 10:
        return x / 10
    if x <= 100:
        return x / 100
    return None

import pytest

def test_clamp_unit():
    assert clamp_unit(0.5) == 0.5
    assert clamp_unit(15) == 0.15
    assert clamp_unit(150) == 0.15
    assert clamp_unit(1000) is None
    assert clamp_unit(-1) is None
    assert clamp_unit(float('nan')) is None
```

What does this do, and what's the subtle correctness problem?

seen = set()
def dedupe(items):
    out = []
    for it in items:
        if it not in seen:
            seen.add(it)
            out.append(it)
    return out


### Response

**Purpose:** Removes duplicates from `items` while preserving order.

**Subtle Correctness Problem:**
- **Hashability:** Fails if `items` contains unhashable types (e.g., lists, dicts) because `set()` and `in` operations require hashable elements.
- **Edge Case:** If `items` is a generator or iterator, it can only be traversed once—this function consumes it entirely, making it unusable afterward.

- Dupont connectors are unreliable for audio circuits due to loose contact and high-frequency signal degradation.

- Impedance mismatch in audio amplifiers (like the MAX98357) manifests as white noise or distortion rather than complete silence.

PROCESS_INSIGHTS:

- Validate component specs (e.g., speaker ohms) before assuming software or firmware faults.

User: every assistant message is getting saved twice in the DB.
Assistant: Sounds like a race. Let me check how messages persist.
Assistant: Found it — _persist_message uses asyncio.ensure_future (fire-and-forget), so _memory_db_ids isn't populated when _enqueue_undumped_messages runs. The processor gets memory_id=None and inserts a duplicate row.

- Identified the root cause as a fire-and-forget async pattern (_persist_message using asyncio.ensure_future) causing a race condition where _memory_db_ids was empty during enqueue.

WHAT_DIDNT_WORK:
- No dead ends or wasted effort were evident; the diagnosis and fix were direct and targeted.

TECHNICAL_INSIGHTS:
- Fire-and-forget async patterns (e.g., asyncio.ensure_future without tracking) can lead to race conditions when subsequent operations depend on the outcome.
- _memory_db_ids must be populated and synchronized before enqueue operations to avoid duplicate entries.
- Async functions relying on shared state (like _memory_db_ids) require explicit coordination (e.g., tracking tasks and awaiting completion) to maintain consistency.
- Making dependent async functions await critical setup steps (e.g., flush_pending_persists) ensures correct ordering and state.

PROCESS_INSIGHTS:
- Race conditions in async code often stem from missing synchronization between fire-and-forget operations and dependent logic.
- Debugging duplicate entries in databases should focus on the timing and state of intermediate variables (e.g., _memory_db_ids) rather than just the final output.
- When refactoring async code, ensure all dependent functions are async and properly await critical setup or cleanup steps.

### Task

Summary: Fixed search returning almost nothing at 31k memories: compounding filters (similarity 0.5 → rank ≥3 → score floor) removed ~85% of candidates. Replaced the rank filter with an importance floor, lowered the similarity threshold, and added a recency boost.

User: search barely returns anything now that the corpus is huge.
Assistant: Your filters compound — similarity 0.5, then rank≥3, then a score floor. On 31k memories that strips ~85% of candidates.
User: what should change?
Assistant: Drop the rank filter for a continuous importance floor (0.25), lower similarity to ~0.35, give FTS-only hits a baseline similarity, and add a recency boost.
